Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, doctor. Thank you so much for fitting me in. I... well, I hope I'm in the right place, and I really don't want to take up too much of your time. 

It's just that I'm usually a very disciplined sleeper. I have to be, for my work. But since my cousin died, I've been having a little trouble. I'm sure it's completely normal to be a bit upset, but I just lie awake replaying that night. And then, if I do manage to drift off, I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning from... just scraps of hospital images in my head. 

I've been going into my board meetings feeling like I’ve only slept in pieces. I always try to keep it together and be professional, but it's starting to make me feel a bit inadequate. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not overreacting by coming in to see you about it.

Well, yes, I suppose I have. I mean, I really try to keep my mind on my work—I pride myself on being focused, you know? But... at odd moments, it just sort of slips in.

But since my cousin died, I've been having a little trouble. I'm sure it's completely normal to be a bit upset, but I just lie awake replaying that night. And then, if I do manage to drift off, I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning from... just scraps of hospital images in my head. \n\nI've been going into my board meetings feeling like I’ve only slept in pieces. I always try to keep it together and be professional, but it's starting to make me feel a bit inadequate. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not overreacting by coming in to see you about it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with sleep since your cousin passed away?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been... about three weeks now. Ever since the night of the, um, the highway collision. I hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill by being here. But yes, it's been just about three weeks.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin's death, have you found yourself having intrusive thoughts or flashbacks about the event, even when you're not trying to think about it?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. It pushes into my mind so suddenly that I actually have to pause for a second just to steady myself. \n\nI always manage to pull it together quickly, of course. I don't let anyone at the office see. But... is that sort of thing normal? I've always believed you just have to push through these things, you know? \n\nI suppose... well, I haven't driven on that particular stretch of the highway since it happened. But that's just because there's a perfectly good alternate route I can take to the office. It only adds a few minutes to my commute, so it just seems practical. And I haven't really been out to dinner with our extended family yet, but that's mostly because work has been so demanding lately. I just haven't had the time. \n\nI'm still functioning perfectly well, I promise. It's just a matter of scheduling, really. That makes sense, doesn't it? I really do try my best to be pleasant with everyone, especially right now. But... I suppose there is one thing. \n\nDuring the prayer gatherings we had, and... well, even when my nieces came over and really tried to comfort me, I just... I don't know. I made sure I behaved properly, of course, and thanked them for coming. But inside, I just couldn't seem to feel any warmth or relief from it. It's almost as if the affectionate part of me has just... gone quiet for a little bit. \n\nI'm sure it's just exhaustion, right? Well, I wouldn't say I'm constantly on edge. I really do try to stay very composed. But... I suppose I have been a bit more, well, cautious lately? 

Especially when I'm driving. It's just... when I'm behind the wheel now, even on the familiar roads I take to the office every day, I find myself constantly checking the mirrors. I keep watching the speed of the trucks around me, and I'm always looking for a safe shoulder or where I could pull off if I needed to. I've noticed my body just stays... tight, I guess you could say. Almost as if I'm waiting for something to happen. 

But that's just being a responsible, defensive driver, right? I mean, it's perfectly sensible to be a little more aware of your surroundings on the road. I hope you don't think I'm overreacting or being paranoid. I just like to be prepared.

Oh, well... I know I mentioned a moment ago about feeling a little quiet inside when my nieces were visiting, but... no, I certainly wouldn't say I'm completely numb. Not at all.

Oh, no, I wouldn't say I blame myself. I mean, it was a terrible accident, and... well, logically, there was absolutely nothing I could have done to change it. I know that. 

I really do try to look at things rationally. I mean, feeling guilty wouldn't be very productive, would it? Oh, um... losing interest? No, I wouldn't say that at all. 

I mean, I'm still very much engaged with my work, and I still make sure to keep up with my usual routines. I haven't dropped any of my responsibilities or stopped caring about the things that matter. It pushes into my mind so suddenly that I actually have to pause for a second just to steady myself. \n\nI always manage to pull it together quickly, of course. I don't let anyone at the office see. But... is that sort of thing normal? I hope it doesn't sound like I'm losing my grip.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of your cousin’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't say I'm avoiding anything, really. I mean, I still go to work every day, I still attend all my meetings. I've always believed you just have to push through these things, you know? \n\nI suppose... well, I haven't driven on that particular stretch of the highway since it happened. But that's just because there's a perfectly good alternate route I can take to the office. It only adds a few minutes to my commute, so it just seems practical. And I haven't really been out to dinner with our extended family yet, but that's mostly because work has been so demanding lately. I just haven't had the time. \n\nI'm still functioning perfectly well, I promise. It's just a matter of scheduling, really. That makes sense, doesn't it? I'm sure most people adjust their routines a little bit when they're busy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood or thoughts since your cousin’s death, such as feeling more irritable, having trouble concentrating, or feeling detached from others?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm irritable or anything like that. I really do try my best to be pleasant with everyone, especially right now. But... I suppose there is one thing. \n\nDuring the prayer gatherings we had, and... well, even when my nieces came over and really tried to comfort me, I just... I don't know. I made sure I behaved properly, of course, and thanked them for coming. But inside, I just couldn't seem to feel any warmth or relief from it. It's almost as if the affectionate part of me has just... gone quiet for a little bit. \n\nI'm sure it's just exhaustion, right? I mean, it takes a lot of energy to keep everything running smoothly at work and manage all the family arrangements. I just hope they didn't think I was being ungrateful. It's completely normal to feel a little drained after something like this, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself being more on edge or easily startled since your cousin’s death, like you're constantly watching out for something bad happening?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm constantly on edge. I really do try to stay very composed. But... I suppose I have been a bit more, well, cautious lately? \n\nEspecially when I'm driving. It's just... when I'm behind the wheel now, even on the familiar roads I take to the office every day, I find myself constantly checking the mirrors. I keep watching the speed of the trucks around me, and I'm always looking for a safe shoulder or where I could pull off if I needed to. I've noticed my body just stays... tight, I guess you could say. Almost as if I'm waiting for something to happen. \n\nBut that's just being a responsible, defensive driver, right? I mean, it's perfectly sensible to be a little more aware of your surroundings on the road. I hope you don't think I'm overreacting or being paranoid. I just like to be prepared.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin's death, have you had trouble experiencing any positive emotions or felt emotionally numb, even during activities or with people that used to bring you joy?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I know I mentioned a moment ago about feeling a little quiet inside when my nieces were visiting, but... no, I certainly wouldn't say I'm completely numb. Not at all. \n\nI mean, just the other day, my management team at the office finalized a rather difficult project, and I was genuinely pleased for them. I made sure to tell them what a wonderful job they did and we celebrated a bit. I really do try to keep a positive attitude, you know? It's so important for me to set a strong, optimistic example for everyone who depends on me. \n\nSo, no, I haven't lost the ability to feel good things. I think I'm just... perhaps a little preoccupied with all the logistics and responsibilities right now, so the joy might just be taking a backseat to getting things done. That's a very normal way to handle a situation like this, don't you think? I'm sure it's much better to stay focused and positive rather than just letting yourself wallow.\n\nDoctor: Have you blamed yourself at all for what happened to your cousin, or found yourself feeling down or guilty about things since their passing?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I blame myself. I mean, it was a terrible accident, and... well, logically, there was absolutely nothing I could have done to change it. I know that. \n\nI really do try to look at things rationally. I mean, feeling guilty wouldn't be very productive, would it? I've been making sure to focus my energy on helping my aunt sort through the estate and making sure the rest of the family has what they need. Keeping busy and being useful is the best way to honor his memory, I think. \n\nOf course, I feel sad—anyone would—but I certainly haven't been sitting around feeling down on myself or letting it get in the way of my responsibilities. I think I've been handling the grief in a very healthy, constructive way. Don't you think it's better to focus on supporting the family rather than getting caught up in all those negative feelings?\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or hopeless for most of the day, even when things were going well around you?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say I've felt hopeless. I really try not to let myself get into that kind of mindset. \n\nI mean, certainly, there are moments where I feel the weight of everything, but a whole day of feeling down? No, I don't think so. I have so many people relying on me at the office, and of course my family right now... I just don't have the luxury of letting myself check out like that. I always try to find the silver lining or focus on the next task at hand. \n\nI think staying busy and keeping a brave face is really the best way to get through these things, right? It's important to keep moving forward. I hope you agree that's a good approach to take.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in things you used to care about, even slightly, since your cousin’s death?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... losing interest? No, I wouldn't say that at all. \n\nI mean, I'm still very much engaged with my work, and I still make sure to keep up with my usual routines. I haven't dropped any of my responsibilities or stopped caring about the things that matter. I think it's just... well, naturally, my priorities have had to shift a little bit right now with everything going on with my aunt and the estate. \n\nSo maybe I haven't had quite as much time for some of my usual hobbies, but it's certainly not because I've lost interest. It's just a matter of managing my time effectively during a crisis. I'm sure you understand how that is, right? It's just about being responsible and putting family first when they need you. That's the right thing to do, isn't it?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, um... I suppose it started right away. The night of the... the highway collision. It's been about three weeks now since it happened. 

I really tried to sleep that first night, I did, but my mind just kept replaying what I had been told. And since then, it's just been a bit of a struggle to quiet my thoughts when I lie down. But I imagine that's a perfectly normal reaction to such a shock, isn't it? I mean, anyone would have a little trouble sleeping for a few weeks after something like that, right? Oh, no, definitely not. I haven't had any days like that.

Oh, reliving it? Um... no, I wouldn't say that.

But yes, it's been just about three weeks.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin's death, have you found yourself having intrusive thoughts or flashbacks about the event, even when you're not trying to think about it?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. I mean, I really try to keep my mind on my work—I pride myself on being focused, you know? But... at odd moments, it just sort of slips in. \n\nLike, if I hear a phone ring late at night, or... or if I'm driving and I see the glare of headlights on the highway. It's silly, I know, but suddenly I get this really sharp picture in my head of the hospital corridor. Or I'll hear the police officer's voice again, just out of nowhere. It pushes into my mind so suddenly that I actually have to pause for a second just to steady myself. \n\nI always manage to pull it together quickly, of course. I don't let anyone at the office see. But... is that sort of thing normal? I hope it doesn't sound like I'm losing my grip.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of your cousin’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't say I'm avoiding anything, really. I mean, I still go to work every day, I still attend all my meetings. I've always believed you just have to push through these things, you know? \n\nI suppose... well, I haven't driven on that particular stretch of the highway since it happened. But that's just because there's a perfectly good alternate route I can take to the office. It only adds a few minutes to my commute, so it just seems practical. And I haven't really been out to dinner with our extended family yet, but that's mostly because work has been so demanding lately. I just haven't had the time. \n\nI'm still functioning perfectly well, I promise. It's just a matter of scheduling, really. That makes sense, doesn't it? I'm sure most people adjust their routines a little bit when they're busy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood or thoughts since your cousin’s death, such as feeling more irritable, having trouble concentrating, or feeling detached from others?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm irritable or anything like that. I really do try my best to be pleasant with everyone, especially right now. But... I suppose there is one thing. \n\nDuring the prayer gatherings we had, and... well, even when my nieces came over and really tried to comfort me, I just... I don't know. I made sure I behaved properly, of course, and thanked them for coming. But inside, I just couldn't seem to feel any warmth or relief from it. It's almost as if the affectionate part of me has just... gone quiet for a little bit. \n\nI'm sure it's just exhaustion, right? I mean, it takes a lot of energy to keep everything running smoothly at work and manage all the family arrangements. I just hope they didn't think I was being ungrateful. It's completely normal to feel a little drained after something like this, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself being more on edge or easily startled since your cousin’s death, like you're constantly watching out for something bad happening?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm constantly on edge. I really do try to stay very composed. But... I suppose I have been a bit more, well, cautious lately? \n\nEspecially when I'm driving. It's just... when I'm behind the wheel now, even on the familiar roads I take to the office every day, I find myself constantly checking the mirrors. I keep watching the speed of the trucks around me, and I'm always looking for a safe shoulder or where I could pull off if I needed to. I've noticed my body just stays... tight, I guess you could say. Almost as if I'm waiting for something to happen. \n\nBut that's just being a responsible, defensive driver, right? I mean, it's perfectly sensible to be a little more aware of your surroundings on the road. I hope you don't think I'm overreacting or being paranoid. I just like to be prepared.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin's death, have you had trouble experiencing any positive emotions or felt emotionally numb, even during activities or with people that used to bring you joy?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I know I mentioned a moment ago about feeling a little quiet inside when my nieces were visiting, but... no, I certainly wouldn't say I'm completely numb. Not at all. \n\nI mean, just the other day, my management team at the office finalized a rather difficult project, and I was genuinely pleased for them. I made sure to tell them what a wonderful job they did and we celebrated a bit. I really do try to keep a positive attitude, you know? It's so important for me to set a strong, optimistic example for everyone who depends on me. \n\nSo, no, I haven't lost the ability to feel good things. I think I'm just... perhaps a little preoccupied with all the logistics and responsibilities right now, so the joy might just be taking a backseat to getting things done. That's a very normal way to handle a situation like this, don't you think? I'm sure it's much better to stay focused and positive rather than just letting yourself wallow.\n\nDoctor: Have you blamed yourself at all for what happened to your cousin, or found yourself feeling down or guilty about things since their passing?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I blame myself. I mean, it was a terrible accident, and... well, logically, there was absolutely nothing I could have done to change it. I know that. \n\nI really do try to look at things rationally. I mean, feeling guilty wouldn't be very productive, would it? I've been making sure to focus my energy on helping my aunt sort through the estate and making sure the rest of the family has what they need. Keeping busy and being useful is the best way to honor his memory, I think. \n\nOf course, I feel sad—anyone would—but I certainly haven't been sitting around feeling down on myself or letting it get in the way of my responsibilities. I think I've been handling the grief in a very healthy, constructive way. Don't you think it's better to focus on supporting the family rather than getting caught up in all those negative feelings?\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or hopeless for most of the day, even when things were going well around you?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say I've felt hopeless. I'm sure it's much better to stay focused and positive rather than just letting yourself wallow.\n\nDoctor: Have you blamed yourself at all for what happened to your cousin, or found yourself feeling down or guilty about things since their passing?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I blame myself. I mean, it was a terrible accident, and... well, logically, there was absolutely nothing I could have done to change it. I know that. \n\nI really do try to look at things rationally. I mean, feeling guilty wouldn't be very productive, would it? I've been making sure to focus my energy on helping my aunt sort through the estate and making sure the rest of the family has what they need. Keeping busy and being useful is the best way to honor his memory, I think. \n\nOf course, I feel sad—anyone would—but I certainly haven't been sitting around feeling down on myself or letting it get in the way of my responsibilities. I think I've been handling the grief in a very healthy, constructive way. Don't you think it's better to focus on supporting the family rather than getting caught up in all those negative feelings?\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or hopeless for most of the day, even when things were going well around you?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say I've felt hopeless. It's not like I actually feel like I'm back in that moment or that it's happening all over again right in front of me. \n\nI know some people have those kinds of intense... flashbacks, I think they call them? But I haven't experienced anything like that. I'm very grounded in reality and focused on the present. I just have some persistent thoughts, which is completely understandable under the circumstances, wouldn't you agree? It's just my brain trying to process the shock of the news, not anything more extreme than that. That's a pretty normal distinction to make, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from your surroundings, as though things around you weren’t real, since your cousin’s death?\n\nPatient: Oh, detached? Um... no, not at all. Like things aren't real? No. I've actually been very... very present through all of this. \n\nI mean, with my role at the company, I really can't afford to just check out, you know? I have to stay sharp for my team, and obviously for my aunt right now. So everything feels very, very real to me. I haven't lost touch with my surroundings or anything like that. I'm staying completely grounded and just handling my responsibilities as they come. \n\nThat's a good sign, isn't it? People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, um... I wouldn't say I've had trouble feeling happy, exactly. That sounds so... cold, doesn't it? 

I mean, I've been going to the prayer gatherings, and my nieces have been so sweet, trying to comfort me and everything. And I'm doing all the right things—smiling, thanking them, behaving exactly how I should. It's just... well, if I'm being completely honest, I haven't really felt that warmth or relief inside when they hug me. It's almost as if the affectionate part of me has just gone a little quiet for now. 

But I think that's just because I'm so focused on being the strong one for the family, you know? I'm sure it's perfectly normal to feel a little numb when you're carrying so much responsibility, right? I just don't want anyone to think I'm ungrateful for their support. I'm handling it the best way I can, wouldn't you say?

I just like to be prepared.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin's death, have you had trouble experiencing any positive emotions or felt emotionally numb, even during activities or with people that used to bring you joy?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... Not at all. \n\nI mean, just the other day, my management team at the office finalized a rather difficult project, and I was genuinely pleased for them. I made sure to tell them what a wonderful job they did and we celebrated a bit. I really do try to keep a positive attitude, you know? It's so important for me to set a strong, optimistic example for everyone who depends on me. \n\nSo, no, I haven't lost the ability to feel good things. I think I'm just... perhaps a little preoccupied with all the logistics and responsibilities right now, so the joy might just be taking a backseat to getting things done. That's a very normal way to handle a situation like this, don't you think? I mean, it was a terrible accident, and... well, logically, there was absolutely nothing I could have done to change it. I know that. \n\nI really do try to look at things rationally. I mean, feeling guilty wouldn't be very productive, would it? I think I've been handling the grief in a very healthy, constructive way. Don't you think it's better to focus on supporting the family rather than getting caught up in all those negative feelings?\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or hopeless for most of the day, even when things were going well around you?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say I've felt hopeless. I always try to find the silver lining or focus on the next task at hand. \n\nI think staying busy and keeping a brave face is really the best way to get through these things, right? It's important to keep moving forward. I hope you agree that's a good approach to take.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in things you used to care about, even slightly, since your cousin’s death?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... losing interest? Well, um... they don't happen all the time, really. I'm usually very good at keeping my mind on my work during the day. 

It's just... sometimes, at odd moments, they sort of pop up. Like if I hear a phone ring late, or if I'm driving and see the glare of headlights on the highway... just for a few seconds, I get this really sharp picture in my head of the hospital corridor, or I hear the police officer's voice telling us what happened. It just kind of pushes into my mind, and I have to pause for a moment just to steady myself. 

But it's very brief, usually just a few seconds, maybe a minute at most. Then I take a deep breath and get right back to what I was doing. That's a pretty normal reaction to have when you're reminded of something upsetting, right? I mean, everyone has moments where a memory catches them off guard for a second, wouldn't you agree?

I mean, everyone has moments where a memory catches them off guard for a second, wouldn't you agree?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these thoughts or feelings about your cousin’s death caused you significant distress or made it hard for you to function in any part of your life, like work or with family?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 58 / chief_executive / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m still showing up for the most essential meetings, but I’ve had to delegate community obligations and shorten my workdays because I’m not as steady or effective with people as I normally am.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, "hard to function" sounds so... severe. I wouldn't say that. I'm definitely still functioning! I mean, I'm still showing up for all the most essential meetings at the company. I wouldn't let things just fall apart. 

But... well, if I'm being completely open with you, I suppose I have had to make a few small adjustments lately. I've delegated some of my community obligations for the time being, and... I've been shortening my workdays just a little bit. It's just that I'm not quite as steady or, um, as effective with people as I normally am right now. I just don't want to be short with anyone or make a poor decision when my head isn't completely in the game. 

But recognizing when to delegate and pace yourself, that's just good leadership, isn't it? I mean, stepping back just a little bit so I don't let my team down is the responsible way to handle this, don't you think?

I mean, it's perfectly sensible to be a little more aware of your surroundings on the road. I hope you don't think I'm overreacting or being paranoid. I just like to be prepared.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin's death, have you had trouble experiencing any positive emotions or felt emotionally numb, even during activities or with people that used to bring you joy?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I know I mentioned a moment ago about feeling a little quiet inside when my nieces were visiting, but... no, I certainly wouldn't say I'm completely numb. Not at all. \n\nI mean, just the other day, my management team at the office finalized a rather difficult project, and I was genuinely pleased for them. I made sure to tell them what a wonderful job they did and we celebrated a bit. I really do try to keep a positive attitude, you know? It's so important for me to set a strong, optimistic example for everyone who depends on me. \n\nSo, no, I haven't lost the ability to feel good things. I think I'm just... perhaps a little preoccupied with all the logistics and responsibilities right now, so the joy might just be taking a backseat to getting things done. I mean, it was a terrible accident, and... well, logically, there was absolutely nothing I could have done to change it. I know that. \n\nI really do try to look at things rationally. I mean, feeling guilty wouldn't be very productive, would it? I've been making sure to focus my energy on helping my aunt sort through the estate and making sure the rest of the family has what they need. I think I've been handling the grief in a very healthy, constructive way. Don't you think it's better to focus on supporting the family rather than getting caught up in all those negative feelings?\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or hopeless for most of the day, even when things were going well around you?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say I've felt hopeless. I really try not to let myself get into that kind of mindset. \n\nI mean, certainly, there are moments where I feel the weight of everything, but a whole day of feeling down? No, I don't think so. I have so many people relying on me at the office, and of course my family right now... I just don't have the luxury of letting myself check out like that. I always try to find the silver lining or focus on the next task at hand. \n\nI think staying busy and keeping a brave face is really the best way to get through these things, right? It's important to keep moving forward. I hope you agree that's a good approach to take.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in things you used to care about, even slightly, since your cousin’s death?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... losing interest? No, I wouldn't say that at all. \n\nI mean, I'm still very much engaged with my work, and I still make sure to keep up with my usual routines. I haven't dropped any of my responsibilities or stopped caring about the things that matter. It's just a matter of managing my time effectively during a crisis. I'm sure you understand how that is, right? It's just about being responsible and putting family first when they need you. That's the right thing to do, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe exactly when the sleep problems and these intrusive thoughts began in relation to your cousin’s death? And since then, it's just been a bit of a struggle to quiet my thoughts when I lie down. But I imagine that's a perfectly normal reaction to such a shock, isn't it? I mean, anyone would have a little trouble sleeping for a few weeks after something like that, right? I'm sure it will pass soon once everything is settled with the estate.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several days in a row where you felt emotionally down or depressed for most of the day, even when you were managing your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I haven't had any days like that. \n\nI mean, of course, I've had moments where I feel sad about my cousin—that's to be expected, isn't it? But feeling down or depressed for most of the day, several days in a row? No, I really don't let myself get to that point. I've been making sure to stay on top of everything at work, and I'm handling all the arrangements for my aunt. \n\nI just keep pushing through and focusing on what needs to be done. I think staying positive and active is really the healthiest way to handle grief, don't you? It wouldn't do anyone any good if I just sat around feeling depressed all day. I'm doing the right thing by staying strong for everyone, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt as though the night of your cousin’s accident was happening again, like you were reliving it in some way, even briefly?\n\nPatient: Oh, reliving it? I'm staying completely grounded and just handling my responsibilities as they come. \n\nThat's a good sign, isn't it? I mean, staying engaged and focused like this... it shows I'm coping with it all in a healthy way, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble feeling happy, even during moments that would usually bring you joy, since your cousin’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I haven't had any days like that. \n\nI mean, of course, I've had moments where I feel sad about my cousin—that's to be expected, isn't it? But feeling down or depressed for most of the day, several days in a row? No, I really don't let myself get to that point. I've been making sure to stay on top of everything at work, and I'm handling all the arrangements for my aunt. \n\nI just keep pushing through and focusing on what needs to be done. I think staying positive and active is really the healthiest way to handle grief, don't you? It wouldn't do anyone any good if I just sat around feeling depressed all day. I'm doing the right thing by staying strong for everyone, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt as though the night of your cousin’s accident was happening again, like you were reliving it in some way, even briefly?\n\nPatient: Oh, reliving it? I'm staying completely grounded and just handling my responsibilities as they come. \n\nThat's a good sign, isn't it? Well, um... I really don't stay upset for very long at all. I mean, I'm very good at pulling myself together.

I haven't dropped any of my responsibilities or stopped caring about the things that matter. I think it's just... well, naturally, my priorities have had to shift a little bit right now with everything going on with my aunt and the estate. \n\nSo maybe I haven't had quite as much time for some of my usual hobbies, but it's certainly not because I've lost interest. It's just a matter of managing my time effectively during a crisis. I'm sure you understand how that is, right? It's just about being responsible and putting family first when they need you. That's the right thing to do, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe exactly when the sleep problems and these intrusive thoughts began in relation to your cousin’s death? Were they present immediately, or did they develop gradually over the first few days or weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose it started right away. The night of the... the highway collision. It's been about three weeks now since it happened. \n\nI really tried to sleep that first night, I did, but my mind just kept replaying what I had been told. And since then, it's just been a bit of a struggle to quiet my thoughts when I lie down. But I imagine that's a perfectly normal reaction to such a shock, isn't it? I mean, anyone would have a little trouble sleeping for a few weeks after something like that, right? I'm sure it will pass soon once everything is settled with the estate.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several days in a row where you felt emotionally down or depressed for most of the day, even when you were managing your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I haven't had any days like that. \n\nI mean, of course, I've had moments where I feel sad about my cousin—that's to be expected, isn't it? But feeling down or depressed for most of the day, several days in a row? No, I really don't let myself get to that point. I've been making sure to stay on top of everything at work, and I'm handling all the arrangements for my aunt. \n\nI just keep pushing through and focusing on what needs to be done. I think staying positive and active is really the healthiest way to handle grief, don't you? It wouldn't do anyone any good if I just sat around feeling depressed all day. I'm doing the right thing by staying strong for everyone, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt as though the night of your cousin’s accident was happening again, like you were reliving it in some way, even briefly?\n\nPatient: Oh, reliving it? Um... no, I wouldn't say that. \n\nI mean, like I mentioned, my mind does replay what I was told about the collision sometimes, especially at night when I'm trying to sleep. But it's just thoughts, you know? It's not like I actually feel like I'm back in that moment or that it's happening all over again right in front of me. \n\nI know some people have those kinds of intense... flashbacks, I think they call them? But I haven't experienced anything like that. I'm very grounded in reality and focused on the present. I just have some persistent thoughts, which is completely understandable under the circumstances, wouldn't you agree? It's just my brain trying to process the shock of the news, not anything more extreme than that. That's a pretty normal distinction to make, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from your surroundings, as though things around you weren’t real, since your cousin’s death?\n\nPatient: Oh, detached? Um... no, not at all. Like things aren't real? No. I've actually been very... very present through all of this. \n\nI mean, with my role at the company, I really can't afford to just check out, you know? I have to stay sharp for my team, and obviously for my aunt right now. So everything feels very, very real to me. I haven't lost touch with my surroundings or anything like that. I'm staying completely grounded and just handling my responsibilities as they come. \n\nThat's a good sign, isn't it? I mean, staying engaged and focused like this... it shows I'm coping with it all in a healthy way, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble feeling happy, even during moments that would usually bring you joy, since your cousin’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say I've had trouble feeling happy, exactly. That sounds so... cold, doesn't it? \n\nI mean, I've been going to the prayer gatherings, and my nieces have been so sweet, trying to comfort me and everything. And I'm doing all the right things—smiling, thanking them, behaving exactly how I should. It's just... well, if I'm being completely honest, I haven't really felt that warmth or relief inside when they hug me. It's almost as if the affectionate part of me has just gone a little quiet for now. \n\nBut I think that's just because I'm so focused on being the strong one for the family, you know? It just kind of pushes into my mind, and I have to pause for a moment just to steady myself. \n\nBut it's very brief, usually just a few seconds, maybe a minute at most. Then I take a deep breath and get right back to what I was doing. That's a pretty normal reaction to have when you're reminded of something upsetting, right? I mean, everyone has moments where a memory catches them off guard for a second, wouldn't you agree?\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts or feelings about your cousin’s death caused you significant distress or made it hard for you to function in any part of your life, like work or with family?\n\nPatient: Oh, \"hard to function\" sounds so... severe. I wouldn't let things just fall apart. \n\nBut... well, if I'm being completely open with you, I suppose I have had to make a few small adjustments lately. I've delegated some of my community obligations for the time being, and... I've been shortening my workdays just a little bit. I really don't stay upset for very long at all. I mean, I'm very good at pulling myself together. \n\nIt's just... maybe for a few minutes after one of those thoughts pops up, I feel a little... unsettled, I guess you could say. My heart might race just a tiny bit, or I might feel a little tightness in my chest, but I just take a moment, have a sip of water, and remind myself that I'm perfectly safe and that I need to get back to work. I don't let it ruin my whole afternoon or anything like that. \n\nI'm usually back to normal in, oh, five or ten minutes, tops. Oh, goodness no. No, I wouldn't say I've ever felt like I was actually back there in the moment. That sounds... well, that sounds quite extreme, doesn't it? Like something from a movie.

That's a very important distinction, isn't it? I mean, just having a vivid memory is quite different from feeling like you're actually reliving an event. I think it shows I'm keeping a pretty good grip on things, wouldn't you agree?

I know three weeks probably isn't a very long time in the grand scheme of things, and I really should be getting back on track by now, shouldn't I? I just... I hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill by being here. But yes, it's been just about three weeks.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin's death, have you found yourself having intrusive thoughts or flashbacks about the event, even when you're not trying to think about it?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. I mean, I really try to keep my mind on my work—I pride myself on being focused, you know? But... at odd moments, it just sort of slips in. \n\nLike, if I hear a phone ring late at night, or... or if I'm driving and I see the glare of headlights on the highway. It's silly, I know, but suddenly I get this really sharp picture in my head of the hospital corridor. Or I'll hear the police officer's voice again, just out of nowhere. It pushes into my mind so suddenly that I actually have to pause for a second just to steady myself. \n\nI always manage to pull it together quickly, of course. I don't let anyone at the office see. But... is that sort of thing normal? I hope it doesn't sound like I'm losing my grip.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of your cousin’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I've always believed you just have to push through these things, you know? \n\nI suppose... well, I haven't driven on that particular stretch of the highway since it happened. I just haven't had the time. \n\nI'm still functioning perfectly well, I promise. It's just a matter of scheduling, really. That makes sense, doesn't it? I'm sure most people adjust their routines a little bit when they're busy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood or thoughts since your cousin’s death, such as feeling more irritable, having trouble concentrating, or feeling detached from others?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm irritable or anything like that. I really do try my best to be pleasant with everyone, especially right now. But... I suppose there is one thing. \n\nDuring the prayer gatherings we had, and... well, even when my nieces came over and really tried to comfort me, I just... I don't know. I made sure I behaved properly, of course, and thanked them for coming. But inside, I just couldn't seem to feel any warmth or relief from it. It's almost as if the affectionate part of me has just... gone quiet for a little bit. \n\nI'm sure it's just exhaustion, right? I mean, it takes a lot of energy to keep everything running smoothly at work and manage all the family arrangements. I just hope they didn't think I was being ungrateful. It's completely normal to feel a little drained after something like this, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself being more on edge or easily startled since your cousin’s death, like you're constantly watching out for something bad happening?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm constantly on edge. I really do try to stay very composed. But... I suppose I have been a bit more, well, cautious lately? \n\nEspecially when I'm driving. It's just... when I'm behind the wheel now, even on the familiar roads I take to the office every day, I find myself constantly checking the mirrors. I keep watching the speed of the trucks around me, and I'm always looking for a safe shoulder or where I could pull off if I needed to. I've noticed my body just stays... tight, I guess you could say. Almost as if I'm waiting for something to happen. \n\nBut that's just being a responsible, defensive driver, right? I mean, it's perfectly sensible to be a little more aware of your surroundings on the road. I hope you don't think I'm overreacting or being paranoid. I just like to be prepared.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin's death, have you had trouble experiencing any positive emotions or felt emotionally numb, even during activities or with people that used to bring you joy?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I know I mentioned a moment ago about feeling a little quiet inside when my nieces were visiting, but... no, I certainly wouldn't say I'm completely numb. Not at all. \n\nI mean, just the other day, my management team at the office finalized a rather difficult project, and I was genuinely pleased for them. I made sure to tell them what a wonderful job they did and we celebrated a bit. I really do try to keep a positive attitude, you know? It's so important for me to set a strong, optimistic example for everyone who depends on me. \n\nSo, no, I haven't lost the ability to feel good things. I think I'm just... perhaps a little preoccupied with all the logistics and responsibilities right now, so the joy might just be taking a backseat to getting things done. That's a very normal way to handle a situation like this, don't you think? I'm sure it's much better to stay focused and positive rather than just letting yourself wallow.\n\nDoctor: Have you blamed yourself at all for what happened to your cousin, or found yourself feeling down or guilty about things since their passing?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I blame myself. I mean, it was a terrible accident, and... well, logically, there was absolutely nothing I could have done to change it. I know that. \n\nI really do try to look at things rationally. I've been making sure to focus my energy on helping my aunt sort through the estate and making sure the rest of the family has what they need. I think I've been handling the grief in a very healthy, constructive way. I always try to find the silver lining or focus on the next task at hand. \n\nI think staying busy and keeping a brave face is really the best way to get through these things, right? Oh, depressed? No, I wouldn't use that word at all. I mean, of course, I've been sad—it's a terrible loss—but I certainly haven't been spending my days just sitting around feeling down for no reason. 

I try to keep a very positive outlook, you know? I focus on the good memories and on keeping things moving forward. That's the right thing to do, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe exactly when the sleep problems and these intrusive thoughts began in relation to your cousin’s death? Were they present immediately, or did they develop gradually over the first few days or weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose it started right away. The night of the... the highway collision. It's been about three weeks now since it happened. \n\nI really tried to sleep that first night, I did, but my mind just kept replaying what I had been told. And since then, it's just been a bit of a struggle to quiet my thoughts when I lie down. But I imagine that's a perfectly normal reaction to such a shock, isn't it? I mean, anyone would have a little trouble sleeping for a few weeks after something like that, right? I'm sure it will pass soon once everything is settled with the estate.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several days in a row where you felt emotionally down or depressed for most of the day, even when you were managing your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I haven't had any days like that. \n\nI mean, of course, I've had moments where I feel sad about my cousin—that's to be expected, isn't it? But feeling down or depressed for most of the day, several days in a row? No, I really don't let myself get to that point. I've been making sure to stay on top of everything at work, and I'm handling all the arrangements for my aunt. \n\nI just keep pushing through and focusing on what needs to be done. I think staying positive and active is really the healthiest way to handle grief, don't you? It wouldn't do anyone any good if I just sat around feeling depressed all day. I'm doing the right thing by staying strong for everyone, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt as though the night of your cousin’s accident was happening again, like you were reliving it in some way, even briefly?\n\nPatient: Oh, reliving it? Um... no, I wouldn't say that. \n\nI mean, like I mentioned, my mind does replay what I was told about the collision sometimes, especially at night when I'm trying to sleep. But it's just thoughts, you know? It's not like I actually feel like I'm back in that moment or that it's happening all over again right in front of me. \n\nI know some people have those kinds of intense... flashbacks, I think they call them? But I haven't experienced anything like that. I'm very grounded in reality and focused on the present. I just have some persistent thoughts, which is completely understandable under the circumstances, wouldn't you agree? It's just my brain trying to process the shock of the news, not anything more extreme than that. That's a pretty normal distinction to make, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from your surroundings, as though things around you weren’t real, since your cousin’s death?\n\nPatient: Oh, detached? Um... no, not at all. Like things aren't real? No. I've actually been very... very present through all of this. \n\nI mean, with my role at the company, I really can't afford to just check out, you know? I have to stay sharp for my team, and obviously for my aunt right now. So everything feels very, very real to me. I haven't lost touch with my surroundings or anything like that. I'm staying completely grounded and just handling my responsibilities as they come. \n\nThat's a good sign, isn't it? I mean, staying engaged and focused like this... it shows I'm coping with it all in a healthy way, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble feeling happy, even during moments that would usually bring you joy, since your cousin’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say I've had trouble feeling happy, exactly. That sounds so... cold, doesn't it? \n\nI mean, I've been going to the prayer gatherings, and my nieces have been so sweet, trying to comfort me and everything. And I'm doing all the right things—smiling, thanking them, behaving exactly how I should. It's just... well, if I'm being completely honest, I haven't really felt that warmth or relief inside when they hug me. It's almost as if the affectionate part of me has just gone a little quiet for now. \n\nBut I think that's just because I'm so focused on being the strong one for the family, you know? I'm sure it's perfectly normal to feel a little numb when you're carrying so much responsibility, right? I just don't want anyone to think I'm ungrateful for their support. I'm handling it the best way I can, wouldn't you say?\n\nDoctor: How often do these intrusive thoughts about your cousin’s accident come up during the day, and do they tend to last for a few seconds, several minutes, or longer?\n\nPatient: Well, um... they don't happen all the time, really. I'm usually very good at keeping my mind on my work during the day. \n\nIt's just... sometimes, at odd moments, they sort of pop up. Like if I hear a phone ring late, or if I'm driving and see the glare of headlights on the highway... just for a few seconds, I get this really sharp picture in my head of the hospital corridor, or I hear the police officer's voice telling us what happened. It just kind of pushes into my mind, and I have to pause for a moment just to steady myself. \n\nBut it's very brief, usually just a few seconds, maybe a minute at most. Then I take a deep breath and get right back to what I was doing. That's a pretty normal reaction to have when you're reminded of something upsetting, right? I mean, everyone has moments where a memory catches them off guard for a second, wouldn't you agree?\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts or feelings about your cousin’s death caused you significant distress or made it hard for you to function in any part of your life, like work or with family?\n\nPatient: Oh, \"hard to function\" sounds so... severe. I wouldn't say that. I'm definitely still functioning! I mean, I'm still showing up for all the most essential meetings at the company. I wouldn't let things just fall apart. \n\nBut... well, if I'm being completely open with you, I suppose I have had to make a few small adjustments lately. I've delegated some of my community obligations for the time being, and... I've been shortening my workdays just a little bit. It's just that I'm not quite as steady or, um, as effective with people as I normally am right now. I just don't want to be short with anyone or make a poor decision when my head isn't completely in the game. \n\nBut recognizing when to delegate and pace yourself, that's just good leadership, isn't it? I mean, stepping back just a little bit so I don't let my team down is the responsible way to handle this, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: How long do you typically feel upset or distressed after one of these intrusive thoughts comes up during the day?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I really don't stay upset for very long at all. I mean, I'm very good at pulling myself together. \n\nIt's just... maybe for a few minutes after one of those thoughts pops up, I feel a little... unsettled, I guess you could say. My heart might race just a tiny bit, or I might feel a little tightness in my chest, but I just take a moment, have a sip of water, and remind myself that I'm perfectly safe and that I need to get back to work. I don't let it ruin my whole afternoon or anything like that. \n\nI'm usually back to normal in, oh, five or ten minutes, tops. That's a pretty reasonable amount of time to need to compose yourself after a startling thought, isn't it? I mean, I think I'm bouncing back from those moments fairly well, wouldn't you say?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt as though the night of your cousin’s accident was happening again, like you were actually back there or seeing it play out in real time, even briefly?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness no. No, I wouldn't say I've ever felt like I was actually back there in the moment. That sounds... well, that sounds quite extreme, doesn't it? Like something from a movie. \n\nI mean, as I mentioned, I do get those very quick, sharp pictures in my head sometimes, or I might remember a sound, but I always know exactly where I am. I'm sitting at my desk, or I'm in my car. I've never lost touch with reality or felt like I was reliving it in real time. It's strictly just a memory popping up. \n\nThat's a very important distinction, isn't it? I mean, just having a vivid memory is quite different from feeling like you're actually reliving an event. I think it shows I'm keeping a pretty good grip on things, wouldn't you agree?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days since your cousin’s death where you felt down or depressed for most of the day, even when nothing particularly bad was happening?\n\nPatient: Oh, depressed? No, I wouldn't use that word at all. I mean, of course, I've been sad—it's a terrible loss—but I certainly haven't been spending my days just sitting around feeling down for no reason. \n\nI try to keep a very positive outlook, you know? I focus on the good memories and on keeping things moving forward. There might be a moment here or there where I feel a little heavy-hearted, but it certainly doesn't last most of the day. I think it's important to stay active and not let yourself wallow, don't you? That's the healthiest way to honor someone's memory, isn't it?"
